11.15 Open Questions / TODOs (Observability & Audit)
Open Questions
1. Sampling vs completeness
○ Where can traces/logs be safely sampled without undermining forensic and regulatory needs?
○ Which event classes (e.g., System Execution, Package/Env, security incidents) must remain unsampled?
2. Telemetry providers & topology
○ Which default telemetry backends to support out-of-the-box for Enterprise vs Local, while staying
vendor-agnostic?
○ How to design multi-region observability topologies under strict residency constraints?
3. WORM & tamper-evidence
○ How strong must immutability guarantees be in Local Mode vs Enterprise?
○ Which combination of hash-chaining, external anchors, and WORM storage is appropriate per tier?
4. Prompt/response logging & privacy
○ Exactly what textual content from LLM interactions is retained vs hashed, anonymized, or discarded, under
different Compliance Packs and tenant preferences?
5. Standard Evidence Pack schema
○ What is the canonical schema for Evidence Packs that can:
§ Be extended per-sector (finance, healthcare, critical infra),
§ Interoperate with external GRC/SIEM tools,
§ Avoid fragmentation?
6. Cross-tenant telemetry for Meta-Stack
○ Governance model, anonymization guarantees, and regulator expectations for opt-in cross-tenant telemetry used
by HyperDaemon and HyperContinuity.
7. User-level observability controls
○ To what extent should end-users control telemetry about their interactions (opt-in/out, redaction requests)
without breaking audit requirements?
8. Cost of observability itself
○ How to meter observability overhead (detailed logs, traces, metrics) in AI Billing & Usage Fabric and optimize it
as part of overall system economics?
9. Regulator UX & explainability
○ What minimum dashboard and query set is necessary for regulators to consider the system transparent and
trustworthy?
○ How much cognitive detail (reasoning traces, canon state) must be exposed in advanced regulatory regimes
(e.g., EU AI Act)?
TODOs
• Define reference metrics sets, dashboards, and alerts per workspace and role.
• Finalize trace schemas and log/event schemas aligned with open standards.
• Implement reference Evidence Pack flows:
○ Release sign-off, incident forensics, regulator audit.
• Codify retention defaults and override rules tied to Compliance Packs.
• Build UX patterns for Record Auditor & Logbook that expose rich trails without overwhelming non-technical users.
12. Performance, Scalability & Reliability
This section defines the non-functional envelope of the OS Dashboard AI Assistant across Local and Enterprise
deployments, with explicit attention to:
• Driver Fabric (OS/SaaS/Data/Research drivers),
• Unix/System Execution Layer,
• Package & Environment Drivers,
• Research/HPC workloads,
• The multi-plane architecture (Data, Control, Governance).
It refines and extends the frozen canon and Technical Spec Sheets as the authoritative NFR layer.
12.0 Purpose & Scope
Purpose
• Define performance, scalability, and reliability requirements for:
○ Interactive UX and assistant flows,
○ Workflow and Capsule execution,
○ Unix/System Execution and Package/Env operations,

• The multi-plane architecture (Data, Control, Governance).
It refines and extends the frozen canon and Technical Spec Sheets as the authoritative NFR layer.
12.0 Purpose & Scope
Purpose
• Define performance, scalability, and reliability requirements for:
○ Interactive UX and assistant flows,
○ Workflow and Capsule execution,
○ Unix/System Execution and Package/Env operations,
○ Research/HPC workloads,
○ Governance & audit surfaces.
Scope
• Applies to:
○ Local Mode: single workstation deployment.
○ Enterprise Mode: multi-tenant, multi-region cloud or hybrid deployments.
• Must be consistent with:
○ Governance & Policy (Section 10),
○ Observability & Audit (Section 11),
○ Driver Fabric (Section 5),
○ Workspaces & Capsules (Sections 7–8).
12.1 Non-Functional Requirements, Performance Targets & SLAs/SLOs
12.1.1 Core Non-Functional Requirements
• Responsiveness
○ Interactive flows should feel snappy for humans; long-running work must be surfaced as jobs with progress.
• Throughput
○ The system must support many concurrent workflows, driver calls, and daemons per tenant.
• Availability
○ Core planes (Control, Governance, Data) must meet high uptime targets.
• Durability & Integrity
○ Critical data (ledger, CIR, policies, audit logs, Evidence Packs) must have high durability and integrity guarantees.
• Predictable Degradation
○ Under partial failures or overload, the system should degrade gracefully, not catastrophically.
12.1.2 Indicative SLO Targets (Enterprise Mode)
• Core API Availability (Control Plane)
○ Target: ≥ 99.9% monthly, stretch 99.95% for Tier-1 and regulated tenants.
○ Includes: dashboard API, orchestration endpoints, driver registry, policy engine, ledger read/write.
• Latency (p95, intra-region)
(indicative, not yet contractual)
○ Simple reads (tasks, projects, CIR search, ledger queries):
§ ≤ 150–250 ms.
○ Chat / plan generation (LLM-backed, no long-running drivers):
§ Orchestrator + policy + context assembly overhead: ≤ 150 ms.
§ Overall plan suggestion (including LLM): ≤ 2–4 s.
○ Short driver invocations (local tools, SaaS/API calls, quick Unix commands):
§ ≤ 1–3 s.
○ Policy evaluation:
§ ≤ 25–50 ms for common allow/deny/transform decisions.
• Data Durability & Integrity
○ Ledger events, CIR, policy/audit logs, Capsule manifests:
§ Target durability: 11-9s or equivalent on replicated storage.
§ Hash-chained append semantics for ledger/audit to make tampering detectable.
• Governance & Policy SLOs
○ Policy engine availability should match Control Plane SLOs for Enterprise tenants.
○ If policy engine is unavailable:
§ High-risk actions must default to “fail-safe” (deny/queue) rather than “fail-open”.
• Local Mode
○ Perceived UI latency: simple views and local reads ≤ 100–200 ms where hardware permits.
○ Offline behavior:
§ Core workflows (CIR, tasks, Master Stack, ledger) usable offline.
§ Remote actions (SaaS, cloud models, sync) are queued until connectivity resumes.
12.1.3 Throughput & Concurrency Targets (Indicative)
• Control Plane: thousands of RPS across tenants, p95 < 300 ms for typical API requests.

§ High-risk actions must default to “fail-safe” (deny/queue) rather than “fail-open”.
• Local Mode
○ Perceived UI latency: simple views and local reads ≤ 100–200 ms where hardware permits.
○ Offline behavior:
§ Core workflows (CIR, tasks, Master Stack, ledger) usable offline.
§ Remote actions (SaaS, cloud models, sync) are queued until connectivity resumes.
12.1.3 Throughput & Concurrency Targets (Indicative)
• Control Plane: thousands of RPS across tenants, p95 < 300 ms for typical API requests.
• Driver invocations:
○ Per-tenant: hundreds–thousands of driver calls/hour under normal operation.
○ Cluster-wide: tens of thousands to millions of driver calls/day.
• Ledger & Observability:
○ Sustains tens of thousands of events/minute on medium-sized enterprise clusters.
Exact targets and SLAs are tiered by edition (personal, team, enterprise, regulated) and must be captured in product-level
SLA documents.
12.2 Load Profiles, Sizing Assumptions & Benchmark Scenarios
12.2.1 Canonical Load Profiles
1. Solo Power User (Local Mode)
○ 1 active human + 3–10 daemons (Archivist, local AIC, small background tasks).
○ 1–3 concurrent driver flows (filesystem, Git, local scripts, editors).
○ Occasional heavy bursts (builds, simulations, ETL scripts).
2. Small Team / Department Tenant
○ 10–50 active users, 20–100 daemons.
○ 10–30 concurrent orchestration flows.
○ 100–1,000 driver invocations/hour (mix of OS, SaaS, Data drivers).
○ Periodic heavy bursts from CI/test and research workflows.
3. Large Org / Multi-Workspace Tenant
○ 100–5,000 users, 500–5,000 daemons.
○ 100–1,000 concurrent plans and workflows.
○ 10k–1M driver invocations/day.
○ Regular Capsule executions across Dev, Research, Cyber, Business, Finance workspaces.
4. Research / HPC-Heavy Tenant
○ Fewer interactive users, but long-running campaigns (simulations, CAD/CFD, model training).
○ High concurrency of HPC jobs; heavy artifact traffic (logs, results).
○ Workload dominated by job submission, monitoring, result aggregation.
12.2.2 Benchmark Scenarios
• BS-1: Interactive Planning
○ Simulate many concurrent user chats performing:
§ Retrieval, policy checks, small driver actions.
○ Measure: end-to-end response p95, context assembly overhead, policy and ledger latency.
• BS-2: Bulk Task & Driver Load
○ Simulate 1k–10k tasks/day with associated driver calls (CI, dev automation).
○ Measure: queue stability, driver throughput, backpressure behavior, error rates.
• BS-3: Capsule Marketplace Load
○ Large number of Capsule executions/day across tenants and workspaces.
○ Measure: Capsule execution engine throughput, registry lookup latency, cold-start overhead.
• BS-4: Environment Convergence
○ Converge 100–1,000 machines to env specs using Package & Env drivers.
○ Measure: package operation throughput, policy evaluation scalability, audit log volume.
• BS-5: Research & HPC Campaign
○ Thousands of jobs launched/monitored over hours or days.
○ Measure: scheduler integration (Slurm/K8s), queue times, result ingestion throughput.
• BS-6: Audit & Evidence Stress
○ Generate Evidence Packs for many releases/incidents in parallel.
○ Measure: ledger/query load, Evidence Pack assembly time, storage IO.
12.3 Scaling Strategies by Plane (Data, Control, Governance) & by Layer
12.3.1 Data Plane
• Storage
○ Horizontally scalable object storage for CIR, artifacts, Evidence Packs, logs.
○ Sharded/partitioned relational or document stores for:
§ Projects, tasks, ledger, policy store, Capsule manifests.

○ Generate Evidence Packs for many releases/incidents in parallel.
○ Measure: ledger/query load, Evidence Pack assembly time, storage IO.
12.3 Scaling Strategies by Plane (Data, Control, Governance) & by Layer
12.3.1 Data Plane
• Storage
○ Horizontally scalable object storage for CIR, artifacts, Evidence Packs, logs.
○ Sharded/partitioned relational or document stores for:
§ Projects, tasks, ledger, policy store, Capsule manifests.
○ Tenant isolation via:
§ Per-tenant schemas/partitions or row-level policies + per-tenant encryption keys.
• Search & Vector
○ Search/vector indices:
§ Sharded per tenant/region or domain (Dev, Research, Writer).
○ Support hot/warm/cold tiers for older data.
• Patterns
○ Event-sourced ledger:
§ Append-only writes; read models built via background projections.
○ Clear separation:
§ OLTP (operational) vs OLAP (analytics, long-term observability).
12.3.2 Control Plane
• API Gateways & UI Backends
○ Stateless; scale horizontally behind load balancers/CDNs.
• Orchestrator & Workflow Engine
○ Stateless orchestration layer drives:
§ Capsule plans,
§ Driver invocations,
§ Daemon scheduling.
○ Worker pools:
§ Partitioned by workload type (interactive, background, HPC dispatch, security daemons).
• Daemon Scheduling
○ Daemons run in dedicated worker pools with:
§ Priority classes (security > governance > planning > analytics),
§ Per-tenant quotas.
12.3.3 Governance Plane
• Policy Engine
○ Stateless evaluation nodes with:
§ In-memory caches of compiled tenant policies,
§ Strong consistency for updates via centralized policy store.
○ Co-located with orchestrator nodes where possible to minimize latency.
• Regulator Fabric
○ Read-heavy:
§ Optimized for large queries over audit logs, Evidence Stores, and ledger.
○ Batch jobs:
§ For heavy regulatory reports and multi-year evidence queries.
12.3.4 Layer-Specific
• Presentation Layer
○ Web frontends + Local Mode UI; minimal state, maximizing client-side rendering where possible.
• Driver Layer
○ Worker pools per driver type and per trust/privilege tier (e.g., System Execution vs SaaS vs HPC).
○ Scale horizontally by adding more workers or host pools.
• Model/Cognitive Layer
○ Model routing across:
§ Premium external models,
§ Local/on-prem models,
§ Efficient smaller models for low-risk tasks.
○ Concurrency limits and quotas enforced by Model Layer in coordination with Billing & Governance.
• Meta-Stack
○ Often batch or low-priority:
§ Runs in separate pools so HyperDaemon/HyperContinuity/Canon of Truth cannot starve core user
workloads.

§ Local/on-prem models,
§ Efficient smaller models for low-risk tasks.
○ Concurrency limits and quotas enforced by Model Layer in coordination with Billing & Governance.
• Meta-Stack
○ Often batch or low-priority:
§ Runs in separate pools so HyperDaemon/HyperContinuity/Canon of Truth cannot starve core user
workloads.
12.4 Driver Performance Characteristics, Latency Budgets & SLAs
12.4.1 Driver Classes & Latency Budgets (Indicative p95)
• Local OS Drivers (filesystem, processes, UI integration)
○ Typical: < 100–250 ms per operation.
○ Heavy operations (recursive scanning, indexing): treated as async tasks.
• Unix/System Execution Layer
○ Short commands (status checks, small scripts):
§ Target ≤ 500–2,000 ms.
○ Medium/long commands (builds, tests, migrations):
§ Treated as jobs, not blocking RPC calls; progress surfaced via events.
• Package & Environment Drivers
○ install/upgrade/remove/env-converge:
§ Seconds–minutes.
○ All operations:
§ Modeled as long-running tasks with:
□ Progress reporting,
□ Idempotent semantics where possible,
□ Clear success/failure classification.
• SaaS / HTTP Drivers (Git hosts, docs, tickets, finance APIs)
○ Typical: 200–1,000 ms (heavily dependent on upstream).
○ Dashboards with multiple SaaS calls must tolerate 2–5 s for composite views.
○ Caching and bulk requests used where possible.
• Data Drivers (DBs, warehouses, search APIs)
○ Point lookups: 10–100 ms.
○ Aggregations / complex queries: up to several seconds, often async; may stream partial results.
• Research & Simulation Drivers (HPC, CAD/CAE, lab gear)
○ Seconds–hours or days.
○ No RPC latency SLO; tracked via job SLOs:
§ Submission latency,
§ Queue time,
§ Completion time.
12.4.2 Driver SLAs & Manifests
• Each driver defines:
○ Expected p95 latency range,
○ Error budget,
○ Retry policy,
○ Circuit breaker thresholds,
○ Resource usage characteristics (CPU/IO/network).
• Planner uses driver metadata to:
○ Estimate plan cost and latency,
○ Prefer faster/cheaper drivers when equivalent.
12.5 Backpressure, Throttling, Prioritization & Admission Control
12.5.1 Backpressure
• Bounded queues for:
○ Workflows,
○ Driver pools,
○ Model calls,
○ HPC dispatch.
• On threshold breach:
○ Lower-priority requests are:
§ Deferred, re-queued, or rejected with guidance.
○ Higher-priority tasks maintain reserved capacity.
12.5.2 Throttling & Quotas

○ Workflows,
○ Driver pools,
○ Model calls,
○ HPC dispatch.
• On threshold breach:
○ Lower-priority requests are:
§ Deferred, re-queued, or rejected with guidance.
○ Higher-priority tasks maintain reserved capacity.
12.5.2 Throttling & Quotas
• Per-tenant and per-user limits for:
○ Concurrent workflows,
○ Driver calls (SystemExec, Package/Env, SaaS, Data),
○ LLM tokens per time window,
○ HPC jobs in flight.
• Quotas driven by:
○ Tenant tier,
○ Contractual limits,
○ Regulatory constraints,
○ Cost guardrails from AI Billing & Usage Fabric.
12.5.3 Prioritization
• Priority classes (example):
○ P0: Security incident response, key policy operations, critical env rollback.
○ P1: Interactive user requests and small Capsules.
○ P2: Routine background jobs (indexing, archival, compliance scans).
○ P3: Large batch analytics, exploratory simulations.
• Schedulers:
○ Guarantee P0/P1 pre-emption where safe.
○ Ensure P2/P3 are not starved over long periods.
12.5.4 Admission Control
• For each new request/workflow:
○ Check:
§ Policy (allowed? risk acceptable?),
§ Quotas (within user/tenant budgets?),
§ Current load (can we accept now?),
§ Cost (within budget/thresholds?).
• Outcomes:
○ ACCEPT now,
○ QUEUED (scheduled later, lower SLO),
○ REJECTED with explicit reason (policy, quota, cost, saturation).
All decisions are logged and, for significant denials, written to ledger for later analysis.
12.6 Reliability Patterns (Redundancy, Idempotency, Retries, Circuit Breakers)
12.6.1 Redundancy
• Enterprise Mode
○ Multiple instances of:
§ Orchestrator, policy engine, search/index, vector store, ledger frontends.
○ Storage:
§ Multi-AZ replication; optional multi-region DR.
• Local Mode
○ Single-node; robustness via:
§ Crash-resistant local storage,
§ Frequent checkpoints,
§ Optional cloud backup/sync.
12.6.2 Idempotency & Exactly-Once Semantics
• All driver calls and Capsule steps carry idempotency keys:
○ Derived from (run_id, step_id, resource_id).
• Ledger:
○ Serves as the canonical record of effective operations.
○ Combined with idempotency keys to avoid duplicate side-effects under retries.
12.6.3 Retries & Timeouts
• Retries:
○ Policy-driven per driver class:

• All driver calls and Capsule steps carry idempotency keys:
○ Derived from (run_id, step_id, resource_id).
• Ledger:
○ Serves as the canonical record of effective operations.
○ Combined with idempotency keys to avoid duplicate side-effects under retries.
12.6.3 Retries & Timeouts
• Retries:
○ Policy-driven per driver class:
§ Transient errors (network flaps, 5xx) get limited retries with exponential backoff and jitter.
§ Non-transient errors (authorization, validation) are not retried.
• Timeouts:
○ Configured per driver type and operation size.
○ Unix commands, package installs, LLM calls all have sensible defaults and tenant-overridable limits.
12.6.4 Circuit Breakers & Fallbacks
• Circuit breakers applied to:
○ External SaaS APIs,
○ Model providers,
○ Internal services (search, vector, HPC dispatch),
○ Specific drivers experiencing elevated error/latency rates.
• When open:
○ Orchestrator:
§ Uses alternative drivers where available,
§ Or gracefully degrades functionality (read-only views, suggestion-only behavior).
• Circuit events:
○ Logged and written to ledger as reliability events.
12.7 Failure Scenarios, Graceful Degradation Modes & Fallback Behaviors
12.7.1 Representative Failure Scenarios
• Model provider outage/degradation
• Policy engine unreachable or misconfigured
• Ledger write path degraded
• Driver outage or misbehavior (SaaS, DB, SystemExec host)
• Package repository outages or corruption
• Search/vector store unavailability
• Storage/DB partial failures
• Network partitions (Local vs Cloud, cross-region)
12.7.2 Graceful Degradation Patterns
• Model provider degradation
○ Fall back to:
§ Alternative providers,
§ Local or smaller models for low-risk tasks,
§ Template-based Capsules where possible.
○ Pause high-risk autonomic behavior; favor human-in-the-loop.
• Policy engine outage
○ High-risk operations:
§ Default to deny/queue and clearly explain.
○ Low-risk reads:
§ May proceed using cached policy (tenant-configurable), with events marked for later review.
• Ledger outage
○ For operations requiring strong auditability:
§ Default to deny/queue.
○ For low-risk actions:
§ Temporary local buffering of events; must be reconciled when ledger recovers.
• Driver outages
○ Mark driver as degraded; orchestrator:
§ Attempts fallback drivers/connectors,
§ Otherwise defers or partially completes workflows with explicit warnings.
• Search/vector outage
○ Fall back to:
§ Reduced capabilities (e.g., basic full-text),
§ Pre-cached knowledge views.

• Driver outages
○ Mark driver as degraded; orchestrator:
§ Attempts fallback drivers/connectors,
§ Otherwise defers or partially completes workflows with explicit warnings.
• Search/vector outage
○ Fall back to:
§ Reduced capabilities (e.g., basic full-text),
§ Pre-cached knowledge views.
• Network partitions
○ Local Mode:
§ Continue fully for local operations; queue remote tasks.
○ Enterprise multi-region:
§ Maintain intra-region functionality,
§ Pause cross-region, strong consistency workflows.
12.7.3 User-Visible Degradation UX
• Clear banners and state indicators:
○ “LLM degraded”, “Policy engine degraded – high-risk actions paused”, “Environment convergence paused”.
• Provide:
○ Alternative manual workflows where possible,
○ Guidance on expected recovery modes,
○ Links to incident dashboards / status pages when appropriate.
12.8 Capacity Planning, Benchmarking & Continuous Performance Testing
12.8.1 Capacity Planning
• Use Observability (Section 11) to gather per-tenant statistics:
○ Active users, workflows, driver calls, model usage, data volume,
○ Incident & Evidence Pack frequency,
○ Research/HPC intensity.
• Derive recommended cluster sizes and configurations:
○ “Small / Medium / Large” profiles,
○ Specialized “HPC-heavy” and “Compliance-heavy” profiles.
12.8.2 Benchmarking
• Maintain standard benchmark suites:
○ Interactive planning,
○ Workflow/Capsule throughput,
○ SystemExec & Package operations,
○ Search & vector queries,
○ Evidence Pack generation and Regulator Fabric queries,
○ HPC job submission & monitoring.
• Benchmarks run:
○ Pre-release in CI/CD,
○ Periodically in staging/production-like environments,
○ On demand for new major tenant onboardings.
12.8.3 Continuous Performance Testing
• Synthetic load generators (per tenant profile) produce continuous lightweight traffic:
○ Used as canaries for performance regression.
• Alerts when:
○ Latency/throughput deviates beyond thresholds,
○ Error rates or saturation metrics exceed baseline.
• Results:
○ Fed back into HyperDaemon, Cognitive Economy Engine, and SRE dashboards.
12.9 Resilience of Unix/System Execution & Package/Env Drivers
12.9.1 Isolation & Resource Control
• Unix/System Execution:
○ Commands executed in:
§ Containers/VMs or restricted OS accounts where possible,
§ With CPU, memory, IO, and runtime limits.
○ Network access governed by Safety Harness and policy.
• Package & Env Drivers:
○ Run under restricted service accounts,
○ Environment mutations are controlled and auditable,

• Unix/System Execution:
○ Commands executed in:
§ Containers/VMs or restricted OS accounts where possible,
§ With CPU, memory, IO, and runtime limits.
○ Network access governed by Safety Harness and policy.
• Package & Env Drivers:
○ Run under restricted service accounts,
○ Environment mutations are controlled and auditable,
○ Only one package manager operation per host at a time (or tightly limited concurrency) to avoid DB lock issues.
12.9.2 Failure Handling
• For SystemExec commands:
○ Strict timeouts, non-zero exit codes fully logged.
○ Outputs (stdout/stderr) stored or digested as evidence.
○ Failures categorized as:
§ Command not found,
§ Permission denied,
§ Resource timeout,
§ Policy-blocked,
§ Unexpected error.
• For Package/Env operations:
○ Detect and flag:
§ Partial upgrades, lock contention, inconsistent dependency states.
○ Environments labeled:
§ healthy, converging, degraded, broken.
○ Guided remediation Capsules offered for degraded/broken states.
12.9.3 Host-Level Safety
• OS Dashboard must not jeopardize host stability:
○ Limit number of concurrent commands per host.
○ Prioritize host critical services over Dashboard workloads.
○ Provide per-host and per-tenant emergency kill-switch to:
§ Halt all automation involving SystemExec and Package/Env drivers.
12.10 Performance of Research & Simulation Workloads (HPC, CAD, Lab)
12.10.1 Workload Characterization
• Jobs are:
○ Long-running, CPU/GPU or IO intensive,
○ Often executed on external HPC clusters, cloud GPU fleets, or specialized lab hardware.
12.10.2 Scheduling & Queuing
• Use drivers to integrate with:
○ Slurm, Kubernetes, proprietary HPC schedulers, CAD automation APIs, lab systems.
• Jobs modeled as:
○ Asynchronous Capsule steps with:
§ Job IDs, queue time, run time,
§ Status (queued/running/completed/failed).
• Concurrency and job limits:
○ Configurable per tenant; enforced to avoid overloading shared clusters.
12.10.3 Data Handling & Locality
• Large result sets:
○ Streamed directly into object storage; avoid funneling through API frontends.
• Data locality:
○ Prefer compute near data; design pipelines to minimize cross-region transfer.
12.10.4 Performance & Reliability Metrics
• Measure:
○ Queue wait times, job runtime distributions, success rates,
○ Cost per job or per simulation campaign.
• Reliability:
○ Idempotent submission and monitoring,
○ Robust across intermittent connectivity,
○ Checkpointing where supported to resume long jobs after failures.
• Research Workspace:
○ Provides dashboards, progress views, and per-campaign Evidence Pack hooks.

12.11 Open Questions / TODOs (Performance & Reliability)
Open Questions
1. Model Routing & Local Models
○ What default strategy should balance latency, cost, and privacy between premium external models and
local/on-device models?
2. Workload Classification & Prioritization
○ To what extent should workload priority be inferred automatically from patterns vs explicitly set via tenant
policy?
3. HPC & CAD Scaling Limits
○ What hard caps on concurrent simulation campaigns per tenant should exist to guard cluster health?
4. Environment Convergence Strategy
○ In production, should convergence ever automatically remove packages, or should removals always require human
approval?
5. Latency vs Accuracy & Approximation
○ For expensive operations (complex search, TRF reasoning), when is it acceptable to serve approximate results in
interactive mode with later refinement?
6. Meta-Stack Scheduling
○ How should heavy Meta-Stack computations (HyperDaemon, Strategy Garden, Canon of Truth refactors) be
scheduled so they do not impact P0/P1 workloads?
7. Tenant-Level Isolation Under Load
○ When a single tenant overloads the system, what isolation/fencing strategies ensure other tenants remain
healthy?
8. Local vs Remote Model Execution
○ What decision policies (and telemetry) should govern routing between local and remote models for different
workspace types?
9. Degradation UX
○ What minimum UX patterns (icons, banners, flows) are required so users clearly understand when they are in
degraded mode and what that implies?
10. P0 vs P1 Failover Requirements
○ For P0 security/governance services, what redundancy and cross-region failover are mandatory vs optional for
each deployment tier?
11. Cross-Region Observability & Performance
○ How much cross-region aggregation is allowed (under residency constraints) while still providing meaningful
global SLOs?

○ Power-user Local Mode,
○ Baseline enterprise nodes,
○ HPC-heavy nodes.
• Finalize per-edition SLA documents (personal, team, enterprise, regulated).

○ Driver versions, Capsule packs, major features.
• Introduce failure injection / chaos testing:
○ For Control, Data, and Governance planes, plus driver pools.
• Create SRE runbooks and remediation Capsules:
○ For common failure scenarios and scaling events, integrated with Observability (Section 11) and Governance
(Section 10).
13. Deployment Models, Infrastructure & Topologies
This section defines how OS Dashboard AI Assistant is deployed and operated across:
• Local (single machine)
• Cloud / Enterprise (multi-tenant cluster)
• Hybrid (on-prem + cloud)
• Edge (agents on remote/field/lab devices)
It explains how the logical architecture (Planes, Drivers, Capsules, Workspaces, Governance) is mapped onto concrete
infrastructure, network topologies, and runtime shapes.
Across all modes, the system remains shape-preserving:
• Same core concepts: Projects, CIR, Ledger, Capsules, Drivers, Policies, Workspaces.

• Local (single machine)
• Cloud / Enterprise (multi-tenant cluster)
• Hybrid (on-prem + cloud)
• Edge (agents on remote/field/lab devices)
It explains how the logical architecture (Planes, Drivers, Capsules, Workspaces, Governance) is mapped onto concrete
infrastructure, network topologies, and runtime shapes.
Across all modes, the system remains shape-preserving:
• Same core concepts: Projects, CIR, Ledger, Capsules, Drivers, Policies, Workspaces.
• Same Planes: Data, Control, Governance.
• Same Driver abstraction: OS/Unix, Package & Env, SaaS, Data, Research, Governance.
Only scale, placement, guarantees, and trust boundaries change.
13.0 Purpose & Scope
Purpose
• Define deployment patterns that:
○ Preserve the logical model across environments.
○ Satisfy security, performance, governance, and regulatory constraints.
○ Enable OS Dashboard to run from a single laptop up to a multi-region regulated cluster.
Scope
Covers:
• Local Mode architecture and offline-first behavior.
• Cloud / Enterprise architecture and multi-tenant cluster patterns.
• Hybrid and Edge topologies (agents, on-prem, labs, plants, factories).
• Network segmentation and zero-trust boundaries.
• Storage, queues, search & vector infra.
• HPC & research cluster integration.
• Configuration, secrets, and credential management.
• Multi-region deployment and DR.
• Upgrade/migration and backward compatibility.
• Inter-OS federation and knowledge-only links.
• Open questions and TODOs for the infra layer.
13.1 Deployment Modes Overview (Local, Cloud, Hybrid, Edge)
Deployment Modes
1. Local Mode (Single Workstation, Offline-First)
○ Everything runs on a single host (laptop/desktop).
○ Storage is local (embedded DB + filesystem).
○ Drivers are primarily local:
§ OS / filesystem / processes.
§ Local Unix/System Execution.
§ Local Package Managers (brew / choco / winget / apt / dnf / …).
§ Optional user-scoped SaaS accounts.
○ Governance is user-sovereign:
§ Policies are advisory but still enforced by the local Policy Engine for safety.
2. Cloud / Enterprise Mode (Multi-Tenant Cluster)
○ Deployed as containerized services (e.g., Kubernetes) in one or more regions.
○ Multi-tenant: many orgs share cluster resources with strong isolation.
○ Drivers include:
§ Shared SaaS integrations (M365, Google Workspace, Git hosts).
§ Org-level System Execution endpoints (bastion hosts, worker pools).
§ Org-controlled Package & Env drivers (IT-governed).
§ Data, Research, Governance drivers.
○ Governance is hard-enforced via the Policy & Governance Engine.
○ Regulator Fabric and Law-of-the-OS run as first-class services.
3. Hybrid Mode (On-Prem + Cloud)
○ Control plane and bulk storage in cloud or central cluster.
○ Sensitive data & System Execution nodes on-prem or in VPCs.
○ Drivers split:
§ Cloud drivers (SaaS, analytics, some data).
§ On-prem drivers (DBs, microservices, Unix hosts, HPC clusters).
○ Governance orchestrates decisions across both; central policies, distributed enforcement.
4. Edge Mode (Remote / Lab / Field Devices)
○ Lightweight agents on edge devices (lab rigs, factory machines, field laptops, embedded devices).

○ Control plane and bulk storage in cloud or central cluster.
○ Sensitive data & System Execution nodes on-prem or in VPCs.
○ Drivers split:
§ Cloud drivers (SaaS, analytics, some data).
§ On-prem drivers (DBs, microservices, Unix hosts, HPC clusters).
○ Governance orchestrates decisions across both; central policies, distributed enforcement.
4. Edge Mode (Remote / Lab / Field Devices)
○ Lightweight agents on edge devices (lab rigs, factory machines, field laptops, embedded devices).
○ Central cluster hosts:
§ Cognitive planning, policy, ledger, marketplace.
○ Edge nodes expose:
§ Local OS and simulator drivers.
§ Lab instrumentation or OT/industrial interfaces.
○ Emphasis on:
§ Intermittent connectivity,
§ Local caching,
§ Deferred sync.
Shape-Preserving Invariants
• Same Control API across modes:
○ Plan submission, driver invocation, Capsule execution, ledger/CIR operations.
• Same governance semantics:
○ Local Mode: same policy model, relaxed defaults.
○ Enterprise/Hybrid/Edge: strict enforcement and auditor visibility.
• Same Driver Fabric, different physical placement:
○ Local: in-process.
○ Enterprise: services & remote agents.
○ Hybrid/Edge: agents plus cloud driver gateways.
13.2 Local Mode Architecture (Single Workstation, Offline-First)
Purpose
Provide the default polymath power-user environment:
• Fully functional OS Dashboard on a single machine.
• Acts as an authoritative project brain even when fully offline.
• Later can sync to an enterprise cluster or federation network.
Key Components
• Presentation Layer
○ Native / cross-platform desktop UI (e.g., Tk / CustomTk / ttkbootstrap or web shell).
○ Panels for:
§ Workspaces (Dev, Research, Writer, Business, Cyber, etc.).
§ Project & CIR views.
§ Capsules & Workflows.
§ Record Auditor & Logbook.
§ Local System/Driver monitor and terminal.
• Application & Cognitive Layer (local)
○ Embedded assistant_core orchestrator.
○ Local Persona and Daemon runner:
§ Sora, Aria, AIC variants, Critic, Archivist, etc.
○ Local Workflow engine and Capsule executor:
§ Executes Capsule graphs using local drivers and local environment.
• Data Layer (local)
○ Embedded DB (SQLite-class) for:
§ Projects, Tasks, CIR index, Ledger indices, Capsules, Policies, Twin metadata.
○ Filesystem directories for:
§ CIR content, artifacts, logs, Evidence Packs, snapshots, env manifests.
○ Embedded search and mini-vector index:
§ Per-user indices over CIR, code, logs, etc.
• Driver Layer (local)
○ OS Drivers: filesystem, processes, windowing, notifications.
○ Unix/System Execution:
§ Direct shell execution, controlled by Safety Harness and Policy Engine.
○ Package & Environment Drivers:

§ CIR content, artifacts, logs, Evidence Packs, snapshots, env manifests.
○ Embedded search and mini-vector index:
§ Per-user indices over CIR, code, logs, etc.
• Driver Layer (local)
○ OS Drivers: filesystem, processes, windowing, notifications.
○ Unix/System Execution:
§ Direct shell execution, controlled by Safety Harness and Policy Engine.
○ Package & Environment Drivers:
§ Wrappers around local package managers (brew/apt/dnf/choco/winget/etc.).
§ Env Blueprints applied directly to local environment.
○ Optional SaaS/Data/Research drivers:
§ Git, docs, DBs, notebooks, etc., if configured.
• Governance & Policy (local)
○ Same DSL as enterprise, but policies are created/managed by the user.
○ Safety Harness applied to:
§ Dangerous commands (rm -rf /, dd, iptables, etc.).
§ Package operations and environment convergence.
○ Local Law-of-the-OS can still record cases and recommendations, but no external regulator.
Offline & Sync Behavior
• Primary mode: fully local.
• Optional sync daemon:
○ Exports CIR/ledger snapshots to cloud storage or enterprise cluster.
○ Mirrors Git repos or artifacts to remote servers.
○ Conflict handling:
§ Local ledger events merged into central when connected; conflict resolution defined in 13.12.
Failure Modes & Recovery (Local)
• Embedded DB corruption → restore from local snapshots and ledger replay.
• Disk full → pause heavy operations (Evidence Packs, env convergence); user prompts for cleanup.
• System Execution or Package failures → env marked degraded; guided repair via Capsules.
• Local Policy Engine failure → default deny for high-risk operations; advisory prompts for low-risk; events logged.
Security & Compliance
• Encryption at rest via OS-level keychain or encrypted vault file.
• No external telemetry unless explicitly opted-in.
• Local policies can approximate compliance packs for self-governance (e.g., personal HIPAA/GDPR posture).
13.3 Cloud / Enterprise Architecture (Multi-Tenant Cluster)
Purpose
Provide a scalable, governed, multi-tenant deployment for organizations with:
• Strong isolation and identity.
• Centralized governance and regulator-grade audit.
• Rich integration with enterprise systems.
Logical Service Layers
1. API & Ingress Layer
○ API Gateway / Reverse proxy:
§ TLS termination, WAF, rate limiting, authn integration.
○ Public endpoints for:
§ UI, CLI, programmatic access.
2. Application & Orchestrator Layer
○ assistant_core orchestration services:
§ Stateless pods handling chat/plans.
○ Workspace services:
§ Dev, Research, Writer, Archive, Cyber, Finance, Twin, etc.
○ Session/state services:
§ Collaboration, presence, notifications.
3. Cognitive & Model Layer
○ Persona & Daemon services (e.g., AIC, Critic, Archivist).
○ Model router:
§ External models vs local/on-prem vs low-cost profiles.
4. Policy & Governance Layer
○ Policy Engine services and PDPs.
○ Regulator Fabric endpoints.
○ Law-of-the-OS & AI Court services (with enhanced controls).
○ Billing & Usage Fabric.

3. Cognitive & Model Layer
○ Persona & Daemon services (e.g., AIC, Critic, Archivist).
○ Model router:
§ External models vs local/on-prem vs low-cost profiles.
4. Policy & Governance Layer
○ Policy Engine services and PDPs.
○ Regulator Fabric endpoints.
○ Law-of-the-OS & AI Court services (with enhanced controls).
○ Billing & Usage Fabric.
5. Data Layer
○ Multi-tenant relational DBs (Postgres-class).
○ Object storage for CIR/artifacts/Evidence Packs.
○ Search and vector clusters.
○ Observability stores (metrics, logs, traces, risk analytics).
6. Driver & Agent Layer
○ Driver Gateways for:
§ SaaS, Data, Workflow, Research/HPC, Governance.
○ Runner pools / agent coordinators for:
§ System Execution & Package/Env on remote hosts.
○ Extensibility for third-party drivers and capsules.
7. Extension & Marketplace Layer
○ Capsule and driver registries.
○ Marketplace API.
○ Sandbox runtimes for third-party extensions.
Planes in Enterprise
• Control Plane:
○ Orchestrator, workspaces, queue processors, driver gateways.
• Data Plane:
○ DBs, object store, search/vector, log & metric stores.
• Governance Plane:
○ Policy, identity, regulator tenants, Law-of-the-OS, billing.
Tenant Model
• Tenants represent orgs (or groups) with:
○ Distinct policies and driver configurations.
○ Per-tenant slices in DB, search, metrics and storage.
○ Optional dedicated clusters for high-sensitivity or regulated tenants.
Failure Modes & Recovery
• Pod/service failures → cluster reschedules; resiliency per 12.x.
• DB / search partial failures → degrade reads and non-critical features; protect ledger writes.
• Driver outages → degrade dependent workflows; use alternative drivers where available.
13.4 Hybrid & Edge Architectures (On-Prem + Cloud, Partner-Linked)
Purpose
Support tenants that:
• Need central cloud or managed control, but…
• Keep sensitive systems and data on-prem or on edge devices.
Hybrid Architecture Patterns
1. Cloud Control, On-Prem Agents
○ Central cluster (cloud / managed) hosts:
§ Orchestrator, Policy Engine, Ledger, CIR, Marketplace.
○ On-prem OS Dashboard Agents:
§ Install on servers, workstations, and internal clusters.
§ Provide local drivers:
□ OS/Unix/SystemExec, Package & Env, DB connectors, internal microservices, lab hardware.
§ Connect back to cluster via outbound, mTLS-secured channels.
2. On-Prem Core, Optional Cloud Assist
○ Entire cluster runs in customer’s data center or VPC.
○ Optional knowledge-only link to external OS Dashboard ecosystem:
§ For shared Capsule templates and patterns,
§ Without exposing raw tenant data.
3. Edge Nodes (Lab / Factory / Field)
○ Thin agents on:
§ Lab rigs, industrial controllers, field laptops.
○ Agents:

○ Entire cluster runs in customer’s data center or VPC.
○ Optional knowledge-only link to external OS Dashboard ecosystem:
§ For shared Capsule templates and patterns,
§ Without exposing raw tenant data.
3. Edge Nodes (Lab / Factory / Field)
○ Thin agents on:
§ Lab rigs, industrial controllers, field laptops.
○ Agents:
§ Execute Capsules locally under policy.
§ Buffer results/events during offline windows.
○ Central controller:
§ Plans & policies, ledger-of-record, aggregated observability.
Planes in Hybrid
• Data Plane:
○ Split between central (canonical CIR/ledger) and local caches on agents.
• Control Plane:
○ Plans from central; execution may be local or remote depending on driver placement.
• Governance Plane:
○ Central policy definitions; local PDPs enforce at agent side with cached rules.
Offline & Intermittent Connectivity
• Agents keep:
○ Local task queues and logs.
○ Cached policies and identity assertions with TTL.
• If offline:
○ Agents may execute within a restricted policy envelope.
○ On reconnect:
§ Events and ledger entries reconciled with central system.
13.5 Network Topology, Zero-Trust Boundaries & Segmentation
Network Zones (Enterprise / Hybrid)
1. Public Edge Zone
○ API gateways / load balancers.
○ Accepts only TLS/HTTPS.
○ WAF, DDoS protection, coarse rate-limits.
2. Application Zone
○ Orchestrator, workspace services, Capsule service, Billing, driver gateways.
○ Limited outward access to Data, Governance, and Driver zones.
3. Governance Zone
○ Policy Engine, Regulator Fabric, Law-of-the-OS, identity sync services.
○ Hardened, least-privilege access to all other zones.
4. Data Zone
○ DBs, object storage endpoints, search/vector clusters, metrics/logs.
○ No direct public access.
5. Driver / Runner Zone
○ System Execution workers, Package & Env runners, HPC gateways, SaaS connectors.
○ Network egress tightly controlled per driver type and tenant.
6. Edge / Agent Zone
○ On-prem and edge agents.
○ Communicate outwards to specific ingress endpoints; no inbound routes required.
Zero-Trust Principles
• All inter-service communication:
○ Authenticated (mTLS, service identities).
○ Authorized (policy-aware, per-service ACLs).
○ Logged for audit.
• No implicit trust based on network location:
○ Segmentation supports containment but policy is primary.
Failure Modes & Recovery
• Misconfigured network policies:
○ Observability surfaces unexpected service connectivity failures.
○ System shifts into “plan-only / limited execution” modes for drivers behind blocked routes.
• Compromised agent:
○ Agent identity revoked; traffic blocked; environment isolated.

○ Segmentation supports containment but policy is primary.
Failure Modes & Recovery
• Misconfigured network policies:
○ Observability surfaces unexpected service connectivity failures.
○ System shifts into “plan-only / limited execution” modes for drivers behind blocked routes.
• Compromised agent:
○ Agent identity revoked; traffic blocked; environment isolated.
13.6 Storage, Queues, Search & Vector Topologies
Local Mode
• DB & Storage
○ Embedded DB for domain data & ledger index.
○ Filesystem for CIR docs, logs, Evidence Packs.
• Search & Vector
○ Embedded full-text search index, optional small vector index.
• Queues
○ In-process priority queues for daemons, drivers, workflows.
Cloud / Enterprise / Hybrid
• Relational DB Topology
○ HA Postgres-class DB cluster:
§ Primary + read replicas across AZs.
○ Multi-tenant partitioning:
§ Per-tenant schemas or RLS + encryption scopes.
○ Optionally separate:
§ Ledger/event store and metadata store.
• Object Storage Topology
○ S3-class object storage:
§ Per-tenant prefixes.
§ Versioning and lifecycle policies.
○ Optional regional buckets for residency.
• Search & Vector
○ Search cluster:
§ Shards per tenant or index type (CIR, code, logs, Evidence).
○ Vector index:
§ Tenant-scoped embedding collections.
§ Strict tenant filters for queries.
• Queues & Streams
○ Job queues for:
§ Capsules, drivers, daemons, HPC dispatch.
○ Stream bus for:
§ Observability events,
§ Record Auditor ingestion,
§ Security & risk analytics.
Failure Modes & Recovery
• DB outage:
○ Failover to replica; may go read-only for some tenants.
• Object store outage:
○ Metadata still available; large artifact operations fail gracefully.
• Queue overload:
○ Backpressure signals; throttle producers; enforce per-tenant quotas.
• Search/vector outage:
○ Fall back to basic or cached results; degrade semantic features.
13.7 HPC & Research Cluster Integration (Slurm, Kubernetes, Custom Schedulers)
Purpose
Integrate with external HPC and simulation infrastructure without breaking OS Dashboard’s plane model or audit guarantees.
Integration Components
• HPC Driver Gateways
○ Services that:
§ Translate Capsule steps into jobs for:
□ Slurm, PBS, LSF, Kubernetes Jobs, cloud HPC APIs, lab frameworks.
§ Handle submit/query/cancel and log streaming.

Purpose
Integrate with external HPC and simulation infrastructure without breaking OS Dashboard’s plane model or audit guarantees.
Integration Components
• HPC Driver Gateways
○ Services that:
§ Translate Capsule steps into jobs for:
□ Slurm, PBS, LSF, Kubernetes Jobs, cloud HPC APIs, lab frameworks.
§ Handle submit/query/cancel and log streaming.
• Cluster-Local Agents
○ Optional agents running inside HPC environment:
§ Handle file staging, job prep, post-processing, and data movement.
Data & Control Flows
• Research Capsule:
○ Defines experiment or simulation DAG.
• Orchestrator:
○ Uses HPC driver to:
§ Submit jobs with environment specs,
§ Monitor status,
§ Collect outputs.
• Data Plane:
○ Inputs and outputs stored in:
§ HPC-local storage (scratch) + mirrored to central object storage (with metadata).
• Governance:
○ Policies control:
§ Who can launch jobs,
§ Queue selection,
§ Resource quotas.
Failure Modes & Recovery
• Job submission failures:
○ Retry or fallback to alternate cluster; log incident.
• Runaway/stuck jobs:
○ Timeouts enforced; job cancellations; environment flagged for review.
• Cluster outage:
○ R&S workspace shows degraded status; scheduling disabled or moved to alternate compute.
13.8 Configuration Management, Secrets & Credential Management
Configuration Management
• Layers
○ Global defaults.
○ Environment (dev/stage/prod).
○ Tenant-level overrides.
○ Project/workspace-level settings (where policy allows).
• Mechanisms
○ Local Mode:
§ Config files + embedded DB.
○ Enterprise:
§ Config service (API + UI) plus env vars & sidecar config loaders.
Secrets & Credentials
• Enterprise / Hybrid
○ Secrets stored in:
§ Cloud KMS / secret manager / Vault / HSM.
○ Types:
§ DB and storage credentials.
§ SaaS/API tokens.
§ SSH keys for SystemExec hosts.
§ Agent bootstrap/revocation tokens.
○ Access:
§ Limited to specific services and drivers via policy and identity.
• Local Mode
○ OS native keychain / encrypted vault.
○ No plain-text secrets on disk.
Policies & Observability

§ SaaS/API tokens.
§ SSH keys for SystemExec hosts.
§ Agent bootstrap/revocation tokens.
○ Access:
§ Limited to specific services and drivers via policy and identity.
• Local Mode
○ OS native keychain / encrypted vault.
○ No plain-text secrets on disk.
Policies & Observability
• Secrets usage is:
○ Logged by category (never contents).
○ Governed by Policy Engine (who can bind which secret to which driver).
Failure Modes & Recovery
• Secret store unavailable:
○ Use short-lived caches for non-critical paths; high-risk operations may block.
• Compromised secret:
○ Automatic rotation and revocation flows; law-of-the-OS incident.
13.9 Multi-Region Deployment & Disaster Recovery Topologies
Topologies
1. Single-Region, Multi-AZ
○ Baseline HA:
§ Services and DB spread across AZs.
○ Good for most tenants; DR within region.
2. Active–Passive Multi-Region
○ Primary region and warm DR region.
○ Asynchronous replication of:
§ DB (with RPO/RTO SLA),
§ Object storage,
§ Search indices.
3. Active–Active Multi-Region (Advanced)
○ Regions serving local users concurrently.
○ Tenants pinned to regions or explicitly multi-region with:
§ Carefully defined consistency models.
Plane-Specific DR
• Data Plane:
○ Backups + replication; ledger replay for reconstruction.
• Control Plane:
○ Infrastructure-as-code for rapid region spin-up.
• Governance Plane:
○ Policy and Regulator Fabric replicated; regulator tenants may be region-specific.
Disaster Recovery Runbooks
• Capsule-encoded DR procedures:
○ Failover steps, validation checks, and rollback strategies.
• Evidence Packs for DR drills:
○ For regulators and internal governance.
13.10 Upgrade, Migration, Backwards Compatibility & Rollback Strategy
Upgrade Goals
• Evolve platform & schemas without breaking tenants or Capsules.
• Maintain replayability of past workflows for audit & backtesting.
Versioning
• Services:
○ Semantic versioning; multi-version client compatibility.
• Capsule manifests:
○ Versioned schema; engine supports multiple versions concurrently.
• Policies:
○ Versioned rules; history maintained in Policy History.
Upgrade Patterns
• Local Mode
○ App auto-update (opt-in).
○ Local DB migrations:
§ Idempotent, logged, with pre-upgrade backups.
•

○ Versioned schema; engine supports multiple versions concurrently.
• Policies:
○ Versioned rules; history maintained in Policy History.
Upgrade Patterns
• Local Mode
○ App auto-update (opt-in).
○ Local DB migrations:
§ Idempotent, logged, with pre-upgrade backups.
• Enterprise / Hybrid
○ Rolling deployments, canaries, and blue-green strategies.
○ Schema migrations:
§ Phased (add fields → backfill → remove old).
○ Capsule & driver migrations:
§ Tooling for manifest upgrade, fallback to old versions if needed.
Rollback
• Service rollback:
○ Maintain previous image versions for quick rollback.
• Data rollback:
○ Use backups + ledger replays; avoid destructive migrations where possible.
• Capsule/driver rollback:
○ Tenant can pin workflows to specific Capsule versions.
Audit of Upgrades
• All upgrades and migrations are:
○ Logged in ledger and Audit logs.
○ Potentially included in Evidence Packs for compliance.
13.11 Inter-OS Federation & Knowledge-Only Links (Deployment View)
Purpose
Allow multiple OS Dashboard instances (different orgs, regions, or operators) to:
• Collaborate at the level of patterns, templates, and meta-knowledge.
• Optionally engage in federated execution (HyperMesh) under strict contract.
Federation Modes
1. Knowledge-Only Federation
○ Exchange:
§ Capsule templates (without tenant-specific bindings).
§ Env blueprints without secrets.
§ Aggregated anonymized observability patterns.
○ Via:
§ Federation marketplace / hub, or
§ Peer-to-peer federation gateways.
2. Execution Federation (HyperMesh / Inter-OS Execution)
○ Explicit cross-OS Capsules steps (e.g., multi-org supply-chain workflows).
○ Each OS instance:
§ Treats others as external tenants.
§ Enforces local policies, Law-of-the-OS, and regulator constraints.
Deployment View
• Each OS instance runs a Federation Gateway that:
○ Authenticates peer OS instances.
○ Enforces outbound/inbound federation policies.
○ Logs all federation traffic for audit.
Security & Governance
• Federation channels always:
○ mTLS, federated identities (e.g., cluster certificates).
• Local policy is final:
○ No remote OS can force actions violating local Law-of-the-OS.
Failure Modes & Recovery
• Federation hub outage:
○ Local OS continues; federation updates apply when connectivity returns.
• Misconfigured sharing:
○ Policy layer blocks, logs incident; Regulator Fabric may engage.

14. Failure Modes, Risk & Resilience
This section defines how the OS Dashboard AI Assistant anticipates, detects, contains, and recovers from failures across:
• Planes: Data, Control, Governance
• Layers: Presentation/UX, Cognitive, Domain, Driver, Infra
• Deployment modes: Local, Cloud/Enterprise, Hybrid, Edge
The goal is not “no failures,” but:
Predictable, explainable, bounded failures with clear recovery paths, preserved integrity, and regulator-grade evidence.
Everything in this section is wired into:
• Observability & Audit (Section 11)
• Security, Policy & Governance (Section 10)
• Performance & Reliability (Section 12)
• Deployment & DR (Section 13)

• Deployment modes: Local, Cloud/Enterprise, Hybrid, Edge
The goal is not “no failures,” but:
Predictable, explainable, bounded failures with clear recovery paths, preserved integrity, and regulator-grade evidence.
Everything in this section is wired into:
• Observability & Audit (Section 11)
• Security, Policy & Governance (Section 10)
• Performance & Reliability (Section 12)
• Deployment & DR (Section 13)
• Meta-Stack & HyperDaemon / Law-of-the-OS (Section 17)
14.0 Principles & Objectives
Principles
1. Fail visibly, not silently
No critical failure should be invisible to users, operators, or regulators.
2. Fail bounded, not catastrophically
Scope and blast radius are constrained by design (per tenant, project, environment).
3. Fail explainably, not mysteriously
Every material failure must be reconstructable via Ledger, Logbook, and Evidence Packs.
4. Fail forward
Incidents become structured inputs into policy, Capsule design, and systemic-risk learning (HyperDaemon,
Law-of-the-OS).
Objectives
• Provide a shared taxonomy for failures.
• Provide early-warning detection and risk scoring.
• Codify recovery strategies as Capsules and playbooks, not ad-hoc scripts.
• Protect CIR, Ledger, Capsule integrity, and evidence.
• Enable BC/DR with measurable RPO/RTO bounds.
• Surface systemic risk to HyperDaemon and regulators.
14.1 Failure Taxonomy (Data, Control, Driver, Governance, UX, Cognitive, Systemic)
14.1.1 Classification Axes
Every failure is tagged along these dimensions:
• Plane
○ Data Plane – storage, indices, CIR, Ledger, artifacts.
○ Control Plane – orchestrator, workflow engine, queues, daemons, drivers.
○ Governance Plane – identity, policy engine, Regulator Fabric, Law-of-the-OS.
• Layer
○ Driver & Execution – OS, Unix, Package & Env, SaaS, Data, Research/HPC.
○ Application/Domain – workspaces, Capsule runtime, workspace controllers.
○ Cognitive – personas, daemons, TRF, planning and reasoning.
○ UX / Presentation – UI, API, interaction flows.
• Scope
○ Local (single task or Capsule),
○ Project,
○ Tenant,
○ Cluster / Region,
○ Cross-OS / Federation.
• Severity
○ SEV-1 (Critical) – data loss, major outage, regulatory risk.
○ SEV-2 (High) – strong user impact, degraded critical functions.
○ SEV-3 (Medium) – localized, recoverable without major business impact.
○ SEV-4/5 (Low/Informational) – minor glitches, cosmetic issues, near-misses.
• Root-Cause Category
○ Resource exhaustion (CPU, RAM, disk, GPU, file handles).
○ Dependency failure (provider, DB, cluster, network).
○ Data corruption / inconsistency.
○ Policy misconfiguration (too strict / too permissive).
○ Misuse / abuse (malicious or negligent).
○ Cognitive misreasoning or misalignment.
○ UX/interaction mismatch.
14.1.2 Data Plane Failures
• Storage unavailability
○ DB or object store outages (Postgres/S3/local FS).

○
○
○
○

Policy misconfiguration (too strict / too permissive).
Misuse / abuse (malicious or negligent).
Cognitive misreasoning or misalignment.
UX/interaction mismatch.

14.1.2 Data Plane Failures
• Storage unavailability
○ DB or object store outages (Postgres/S3/local FS).
○ Search/vector index nodes offline.
• Data corruption / inconsistency
○ Partial writes, schema mismatches, broken foreign keys.
○ Indexes out of sync with CIR or Ledger.
○ Broken Capsule manifests or env specs.
• Data loss / truncation
○ Misconfigured retention or incomplete backups.
○ Accidental or malicious deletion.
• Classification / residency violations
○ Data stored in wrong region, bucket, or encryption state.
14.1.3 Control Plane Failures
• Planner / orchestrator failures
○ AIC/persona unable to produce valid plan.
○ Plans that cannot be executed (no drivers, conflicting constraints).
○ Model provider outages impacting planning.
• Workflow engine failures
○ Hung workflows, stuck state transitions, mis-sequenced Capsule steps.
○ Deadlocks or livelocks in complex automation graphs.
• Daemon / scheduler failures
○ Daemons not running, over-triggered, or starved.
○ Duplicate or conflicting daemons acting on same project.
14.1.4 Driver & Execution Failures
• Driver unavailability
○ SaaS/API outages, OS/FS driver errors, unreachable DBs, HPC schedulers down.
• Driver misconfiguration
○ Bad credentials, wrong endpoints, missing permissions, wrong tenant context.
• System Execution Layer failures
○ Non-zero exit codes, timeouts, sandbox violations, resource limit breaches.
• Package & Env driver failures
○ Failed installs/upgrades, half-applied changes, broken package DB, env drift.
• Edge/agent failures
○ Agents offline, out of date, or mis-registered.
14.1.5 Governance & Policy Failures
• Over-restrictive policies
○ Legitimate operations blocked, leading to productivity loss.
• Under-restrictive / missing policies
○ Dangerous operations allowed, potential breaches/regulatory issues.
• Conflicting policies
○ Multiple policies producing contradictory decisions (deadlock, inconsistent behavior).
• Regulator Fabric failures
○ Evidence Packs cannot be generated or served.
○ Attestations invalid or missing.
• Law-of-the-OS breakdowns
○ Inability to adjudicate disputes due to missing or ambiguous rules.
14.1.6 UX & Interaction Failures
• Mismatched expectations
○ Users believe an action is safe or small in scope when it is large or risky.
• Silent or opaque failures
○ Errors not surfaced or surfaced without actionable guidance.

• Law-of-the-OS breakdowns
○ Inability to adjudicate disputes due to missing or ambiguous rules.
14.1.6 UX & Interaction Failures
• Mismatched expectations
○ Users believe an action is safe or small in scope when it is large or risky.
• Silent or opaque failures
○ Errors not surfaced or surfaced without actionable guidance.
• Alert overload
○ Too many low-value alerts; users begin ignoring real issues.
• Collisions with automation
○ Users editing resources while Capsules simultaneously act on them, leading to confusion or conflicts.
14.1.7 Cognitive, Meta-Stack & Systemic Failures
• Cognitive & Model-level
○ Hallucination or fabrication in plans/explanations.
○ Goal misalignment (local optimization vs tenant goals).
○ Temporal incoherence (ignoring important history/archive).
○ Cross-persona conflict (e.g., Sora vs Aria vs AIC producing incompatible strategies).
• Systemic & Meta-Stack
○ Cascading failures via capsule networks or HyperMesh.
○ Emergent fragility in HyperFoundry or HyperSymphony automation.
○ Cross-OS federation failures (inconsistent assumptions between OS instances).
14.2 Detection Mechanisms, Early-Warning Signals & Risk Scoring
14.2.1 Telemetry & Evidence Sources
• Observability stack (Section 11)
○ Metrics: error rates, latencies, queue depths, resource saturation.
○ Logs: driver/system execution logs, policy logs, security logs.
○ Traces: workflow spans, driver spans, model spans.
• Ledger & Logbook
○ Event patterns: repeated failures, rollbacks, policy overrides.
○ Divergence between planned vs executed workflows.
• Governance & Security
○ Policy decision distributions (ALLOW/DENY/OVERRIDE spikes).
○ Security monitor signals: anomalies, brute force patterns, suspect commands.
• Cognitive Signals
○ AIC confidence/uncertainty tags.
○ Critic/Oracle annotations.
○ Alignment Monitor flags.
• User Signals
○ Explicit incident reports, “panic/undo” usage, user feedback.
14.2.2 Detection Mechanisms
• Threshold & SLO-based
○ Per-driver, per-service thresholds.
○ SLO burn-rate alerts for key APIs and workflows.
• Statistical & ML-based
○ Time-series anomalies on error rates, latency, cost, env drift.
○ Behavioral anomalies (unusual command/driver usage, suspicious access patterns).
• Policy-based
○ Rule engines watching sequences of events:
§ e.g., multiple denied commands, repeated env convergence failures.
• Causal & Graph-based
○ Use the Ledger’s causal graph to identify recurring failure motifs:
§ e.g., particular Capsule → driver → env combination strongly correlated with incidents.
14.2.3 Risk Scoring
• Dimensions
○ Impact: data sensitivity, infra criticality, user/regulator impact.
○ Likelihood: frequency of failures/near-misses, trend over time.
○ Scope: local vs tenant-wide vs multi-tenant vs cross-OS.

○ Use the Ledger’s causal graph to identify recurring failure motifs:
§ e.g., particular Capsule → driver → env combination strongly correlated with incidents.
14.2.3 Risk Scoring
• Dimensions
○ Impact: data sensitivity, infra criticality, user/regulator impact.
○ Likelihood: frequency of failures/near-misses, trend over time.
○ Scope: local vs tenant-wide vs multi-tenant vs cross-OS.
○ Control strength: policy coverage, monitoring depth, testing maturity.
• Risk Scores
○ Calculated per:
§ Driver, Capsule, environment, project, workspace, tenant, OS instance.
○ Aggregated to:
§ Tenant risk posture, workspace risk posture, systemic risk (for HyperDaemon).
• Risk Bands
○ Green – nominal.
○ Yellow – degraded; extra monitoring, soft limits.
○ Orange – serious; throttling, additional approvals, partial automation suspension.
○ Red – critical; kill-switches, strict containment, P0 escalation.
Risk scores and bands feed into Policy Engine decisions, scheduling and throttling, HyperDaemon, and Regulator views.
14.3 Recovery Strategies & Playbooks (Per Plane, Layer, Workspace)
Recovery is expressed as Capsules and runbooks (CIR + automation), not raw scripts.
14.3.1 General Recovery Principles
• Every major failure type has one or more Recovery Capsules and associated runbooks.
• Recovery actions are:
○ Logged in Ledger and Logbook.
○ Policy-gated where they change external state.
○ Rehearsable in sandbox (Temporal Backtesting) for high-risk paths.
14.3.2 Data Plane Recovery
• Storage Failover (Enterprise)
○ Promote replicas, re-route traffic.
○ Switch affected services to read-only if write path is suspect.
• Index Rebuild
○ Rebuild search/vector indices from:
§ CIR snapshots, Ledger events, Capsule metadata.
○ Run as long-running Capsules with progress reporting.
• Data Repair
○ Schema migration Capsules to re-align schema drift.
○ Data repair Capsules combining:
§ Automated fixes (TRF/AIC suggestions),
§ Human approvals for high-impact changes.
• Local Mode
○ Restore from local snapshots; optionally import from external backups.
○ Offer “open read-only” mode for partially corrupted DBs to salvage content.
14.3.3 Control Plane Recovery
• Plan Regeneration
○ On workflow failure, regenerate plans using updated constraints, live driver health, and error details.
○ Optionally require human approval for new plan.
• Workflow Restart & Compensation
○ Idempotent steps: safe retries to completion.
○ Non-idempotent steps: explicit compensating steps (e.g., revert env changes, roll back deployment).
• Daemon & Scheduler Recovery
○ Daemons with heartbeats and state snapshots.
○ On failure, replay missed events from Ledger/queues.
14.3.4 Driver & Execution Recovery
• Driver Failover
○ Use alternative drivers:
§ Mirror endpoints, backup provider, secondary region, local vs cloud.
○ Degraded mode:

• Daemon & Scheduler Recovery
○ Daemons with heartbeats and state snapshots.
○ On failure, replay missed events from Ledger/queues.
14.3.4 Driver & Execution Recovery
• Driver Failover
○ Use alternative drivers:
§ Mirror endpoints, backup provider, secondary region, local vs cloud.
○ Degraded mode:
§ Provide read-only views, manual instructions when automation is unavailable.
• System Execution Layer
○ Bounded retries with backoff.
○ Host isolation if systemic issues detected (sandbox break, repeated resource exhaustion).
○ Command quarantine lists for high-risk patterns after incidents.
• Package & Environment Drivers
○ Rollback using package manager history where supported.
○ Env convergence Capsules:
§ Reapply known-good Environment Blueprints.
○ Mark env states: healthy, converging, degraded, broken, with guidance.
14.3.5 Governance & Policy Recovery
• Policy Rollback
○ Versioned policies, with:
§ “Rollback to version N” action Capsule.
○ Dry-run simulation of new policies on historical logs via Temporal Backtesting.
• Governance Degradation Modes
○ Policy Engine outage:
§ Strict tenants: fail-closed for high-risk writes; read-only for most.
§ Less strict: allow low-risk reads & some writes under cached policies; log aggressively.
○ Regulator Fabric outage:
§ Internal logging continues; external access paused, but Evidence Packs still producible once back.
14.3.6 Workspace-Specific Recovery Patterns
• Dev / DevOps
○ Deployment rollback Capsules.
○ Test pipeline repair.
○ Env Blueprint reapply and infra drift correction.
• Research & Simulation
○ Job re-submission with corrected parameters/env.
○ Mark invalid runs and attach context.
○ Use env snapshots to re-build reproducible setups.
• Writer & Content
○ Versioned drafts and document history.
○ Conflict resolution Capsules for multi-author edits.
• Business / Finance
○ Reconciliation Capsules for corrections (double-entry methods).
○ Regenerated reports with explicit “correction” tags.
• Cybersecurity
○ Incident response Capsules:
§ Isolate systems, rotate keys, patch envs, collect forensics.
○ Post-incident remediation and hardening Capsules.
• Archive & Continuity
○ Rebuild indices from raw ledger/CIR if Archive indices corrupted.
○ Tag compromised ranges; serve with warnings.
14.4 Data Loss, Corruption & Integrity Protections (Ledger, CIR, Capsules)
14.4.1 Integrity Mechanisms
• Ledger
○ Append-only, hash-chained events per project/tenant.
○ Periodic Merkle roots anchored to external trust anchors (optional).
• CIR
○ Versioned docs/blocks, content hashes and checksums.
○ Optional digital signatures for high-assurance contexts.

14.4 Data Loss, Corruption & Integrity Protections (Ledger, CIR, Capsules)
14.4.1 Integrity Mechanisms
• Ledger
○ Append-only, hash-chained events per project/tenant.
○ Periodic Merkle roots anchored to external trust anchors (optional).
• CIR
○ Versioned docs/blocks, content hashes and checksums.
○ Optional digital signatures for high-assurance contexts.
• Capsules & Env Manifests
○ Content-addressed identifiers.
○ Signed code bundles and manifests for curated Marketplace/enterprise Capsules.
14.4.2 Backup, Retention & Legal Hold
• Local Mode
○ Encrypted local snapshots; user-managed backups to external drives/cloud.
○ UI hints for backup hygiene.
• Enterprise Mode
○ Periodic full + incremental backups.
○ Tenant-specific retention policies, influenced by Compliance Packs.
○ Legal hold mechanisms preventing deletion of specific histories.
14.4.3 Corruption Detection & Recovery
• Periodic structural audits:
○ Ledger chain verification,
○ Referential integrity checks,
○ Capsule manifest validation.
• On detection:
○ Segregate corrupted segments and mark them clearly.
○ Attempt reconstruction from replicas/backups.
○ For irrecoverable corruption:
§ Create “damaged segment” artifacts and Evidence Packs for regulators and Law-of-the-OS.
14.5 Security Incidents, Abuse Scenarios & Containment Strategies
14.5.1 Threat & Abuse Scenarios
• Credential theft, API key compromise.
• Malicious Capsules, plugins, or drivers.
• Abuse of System Execution and Package drivers.
• Prompt injection / model misuse to attempt policy bypass.
• Data exfiltration via drivers or federation links.
14.5.2 Detection
• Security Monitor & Cybersecurity Workspace:
○ Anomaly detection on commands, package operations, network paths.
• Policy anomalies:
○ Surges in denies, suspicious allow patterns.
• HyperDaemon:
○ Systemic risk patterns hinting at ongoing or pending attacks.
14.5.3 Containment & Response
• Safety Harness Activation
○ Hard block dangerous commands (e.g., rm, dd, netsh, iptables).
○ Emergency profiles that drastically restrict drivers.
• Account & Credential Containment
○ Lock accounts, revoke tokens, rotate secrets.
○ Tenant-wide or host-specific kill switches.
• Capsule / Driver Quarantine
○ Disable suspect Capsules or drivers globally or per-tenant.
○ Remove from Marketplace visibility pending investigation.
• Network & Host Isolation
○ Isolate nodes via network segmentation rules.
○ Turn off System Execution on suspect agents.
14.5.4 Post-Incident Forensics & Hardening

○ Tenant-wide or host-specific kill switches.
• Capsule / Driver Quarantine
○ Disable suspect Capsules or drivers globally or per-tenant.
○ Remove from Marketplace visibility pending investigation.
• Network & Host Isolation
○ Isolate nodes via network segmentation rules.
○ Turn off System Execution on suspect agents.
14.5.4 Post-Incident Forensics & Hardening
• Auto-generate Security Incident Evidence Packs:
○ Logs, traces, ledger slices, env snapshots, Capsule code.
• Law-of-the-OS & AI Court:
○ Attribute responsibility,
○ Decide on mitigations (deprecate Capsule, enforce new guardrails, etc.).
• Update:
○ Policies & Safety Harness rules,
○ Marketplace vetting standards,
○ Capsule test requirements and certifications.
14.6 Business Continuity, Disaster Recovery & DR Exercises
14.6.1 BC / DR Objectives
• Establish per-tier RPO/RTO targets for:
○ Ledger, CIR, Policy, Capsule store, DBs, and critical drivers.
• Classify services:
○ Tier-0: Policy, Ledger, Identity, Core storage.
○ Tier-1: Orchestrator, Workspaces, Driver gateways.
○ Tier-2+: Meta-Stack, non-critical analytics.
14.6.2 Continuity Modes
• Read-Only Continuity
○ CIR & Ledger readable; no destructive operations.
○ Default during data-plane uncertainty.
• Automation-Off Continuity
○ Suggest & plan only; drivers disabled or heavily constrained.
• Full Continuity
○ Normal operations under nominal conditions.
Tenants and regulators must always know which mode is active.
14.6.3 DR Topologies & Procedures
• Local Mode:
○ Export/import flows to restore into new installations.
○ “Safe read-only” open mode for damaged installs.
• Enterprise / Hybrid:
○ Multi-AZ / multi-region patterns (Section 13.9).
○ Runbooks (Capsules) for:
§ Region failover,
§ DB restore,
§ Search/index rebuild,
§ Re-pointing agents/edges.
14.6.4 DR Exercises & Validation
• Regular game days:
○ Simulate region loss, key service failures, and driver outages.
○ Validate runbooks, measure RPO/RTO.
• Regulator drills:
○ Demonstrate DR capabilities to regulators using Evidence Packs.
14.7 Systemic Risk Modeling & Inputs for HyperDaemon
14.7.1 Purpose
HyperDaemon acts as the systemic risk sentinel, looking beyond individual incidents to:
• Identify fragile structures,
• Detect emerging systemic vulnerabilities,
• Recommend structural mitigations.
14.7.2 Inputs

○ Demonstrate DR capabilities to regulators using Evidence Packs.
14.7 Systemic Risk Modeling & Inputs for HyperDaemon
14.7.1 Purpose
HyperDaemon acts as the systemic risk sentinel, looking beyond individual incidents to:
• Identify fragile structures,
• Detect emerging systemic vulnerabilities,
• Recommend structural mitigations.
14.7.2 Inputs
• Aggregated, anonymized telemetry:
○ Cross-tenant error/failure patterns.
• Capsule & dependency graphs:
○ Which Capsules, envs, or drivers are central in many workflows.
• Policy & configuration metadata:
○ Weak or minimal setups, repeated overrides.
• Temporal Backtesting results:
○ Counterfactual scenarios showing where smaller changes could have avoided major incidents.
• Federation topology:
○ Cross-OS flows and HyperMesh structures.
14.7.3 Systemic Risk Scenarios
• Single Capsule or driver widely used, with increasing failure rate.
• Common package or env spec causing failures across tenants.
• Cross-region or cross-OS workflows forming unrecognized feedback loops.
• Policy changes that globally increase risk exposure.
14.7.4 HyperDaemon Outputs
• Risk map of:
○ Drivers, Capsules, env specs, tenants, OS instances.
• Recommendations:
○ Deprecate/patch Capsules, require tests, add redundancies, tighten policies.
• Signals into:
○ Policy Engine (risk-dependent rules),
○ Law-of-the-OS (systemic decisions),
○ Meta-Design Studio (architecture improvements).
14.8 Interplay with Policy Engine, Regulator Fabric & Law-of-the-OS
14.8.1 Policy Engine as Risk Gate
• Preventive control:
○ Enforce policies for all state-changing actions.
○ Use risk scores and bands to modulate required approvals.
• Reactive control:
○ Switch system into more conservative modes upon risk escalation.
○ Temporarily ban or restrict risky drivers/Capsules.
14.8.2 Regulator Fabric
• Read-only or supervised access to:
○ Incident logs, DR exercises, Evidence Packs, systemic-risk reports.
• Regulation Capsules:
○ Encapsulate compliance checks for incident response, DR readiness, resilience standards.
14.8.3 Law-of-the-OS & AI Court
• Adjudicates:
○ Complex incidents and failures:
§ Human misconfig vs AI misbehavior vs Capsule/driver defects vs policy gaps.
• Produces:
○ Precedents and rulings that:
§ Influence default policies,
§ Drive Capsule certification criteria,
§ Inform Marketplace curation and Meta-Stack behavior.
• Guarantees:
○ Failures feed into system improvement, not just “closed incidents”.
14.9 Open Questions / TODOs (Failure & Risk)
1. Formal Risk Models vs Heuristics

○ Precedents and rulings that:
§ Influence default policies,
§ Drive Capsule certification criteria,
§ Inform Marketplace curation and Meta-Stack behavior.
• Guarantees:
○ Failures feed into system improvement, not just “closed incidents”.
14.9 Open Questions / TODOs (Failure & Risk)
1. Formal Risk Models vs Heuristics
○ How soon to adopt quantitative risk models (Bayesian, credit-style scoring) vs simpler heuristic scoring.
2. Tenant-Level Risk Preferences
○ How to surface configurable risk appetite and map it to policies and automation limits.
3. Cross-Tenant Telemetry & Privacy
○ Legal and technical frameworks for HyperDaemon’s cross-tenant systemic risk analysis.
4. Minimum Viable Law-of-the-OS
○ What is required for v1 vs later expansions, especially in unregulated vs heavily regulated sectors.
5. Chaos & Failure Injection
○ How aggressive built-in chaos testing should be in production vs staging.
6. CIR/Ledger Integrity in Air-Gapped / Edge Contexts
○ How to guarantee or approximate integrity when connectivity is intermittent or absent.
7. Automatic vs Human-Approved Self-Healing
○ How to parameterize the level of autonomous remediation allowed per tenant and per risk band.
8. Standard Incident & DR Capsule Library
○ Which incident types must have first-class Canonical Capsules by default (e.g., DB outage, driver compromise,
region loss).
9. Federation Failure Contracts
○ Exact semantics and SLAs when failures propagate across federated OS instances.
This section defines the risk and resilience backbone that the rest of the OS Dashboard canon relies on. All implementation
work in drivers, Capsules, workspaces, Meta-Stack, and deployment must treat this as the non-negotiable envelope for
responsible, governable failure.
15. AI Billing, Cost Governance, Economics & Strategic Value Envelope
15.0 Purpose & Scope
This section defines how the OS Dashboard turns raw compute, drivers, Capsules, and Meta-Stack capabilities into priced,
governed economic surfaces.
It specifies:
• The AI Billing & Usage Fabric – driver-first metering, attribution, and rating.
• Budgets, quotas, and economic guardrails integrated with the Policy Engine.
• Multi-tenant billing, chargeback, invoicing, and revenue share for Marketplace assets.
• Marketplace monetization models for Drivers, Capsule Packs, Vertical OS bundles, and Meta-Stack layers.
• Higher-order economic orchestration engines:
○ Strategy Garden & Capsule Fund – strategic capital allocation to Capsule graphs.
○ Cognitive Economy Engine – allocation of work, attention, and compute across humans, agents, Capsules, and
infra.
This layer is tightly coupled with:
• Policy & Governance Engine (Section 10).
• Observability & Usage (Sections 6 & 11).
• Capsules & Marketplace (Sections 8–9).
• Meta-Stack capabilities (Section 17).
The design is consistent with the driver-first billing architecture and with the frozen canon spec.
15.1 Product Surface Areas, Editions & Packaging
15.1.1 Purpose / Responsibilities
Define how the platform is packaged, licensed, and priced, and how those SKUs map to:
• Technical capabilities (drivers, workspaces, Capsules, Meta-Stack layers).
• Governance capabilities (compliance packs, Regulator Fabric, Law-of-the-OS).
• Economic controls (budgets, quotas, pricing_plans).
15.1.2 Editions & SKUs
Core OS Editions
• Local / Solo OS
○ Local Mode, single user, limited drivers and Capsule count.
○ Optional connection to cloud services for models and sync.
• Team / Pro OS
○ Multi-user tenant, shared workspaces, shared Archive, shared Capsule library.
• Enterprise OS
○ Full governance, Policy Engine, Regulator Fabric hooks, DR & continuity, private Marketplace.

○ Edition (core, pro, enterprise).

○ Promotional/contractual plan (e.g., enterprise_tier_2, academic_research).

○ Compute charges (Rating Engine).
○ Enforce edition boundaries (Policy Engine).
